`docs/API.md`:

```md
# API Reference

```ts
import Security from '@rs-native-kit/security';
// or, tree-shakeable named imports:
import { isRooted, scan, addListener } from '@rs-native-kit/security';
```

All detection methods are `async` and return `Promise<boolean>` (or `Promise<string>` for hashes). They run off the JS thread on both platforms (Kotlin `Promise.parallel` / Swift `Promise.parallel`), so they never block rendering.

## Individual checks

| Method | Android | iOS | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `isRooted()` | ✅ | always `false` | Root is an Android-only concept |
| `isMagiskDetected()` | ✅ | always `false` | |
| `isBusyBoxDetected()` | ✅ | always `false` | |
| `isJailbroken()` | always `false` | ✅ | Jailbreak is an iOS-only concept |
| `isEmulator()` | ✅ | ✅ (Simulator) | 100% accurate on iOS (compile-time flag); heuristic on Android |
| `isDeveloperModeEnabled()` | ✅ | best-effort | iOS: embedded provisioning profile presence |
| `isUsbDebuggingEnabled()` | ✅ | always `false` | No iOS equivalent (Xcode uses a paired trust relationship, not a toggle) |
| `isDebuggerAttached()` | ✅ | ✅ | Native debugger attach, independent of the JS debugger |
| `isMockLocationEnabled()` | ✅ | best-effort | iOS: only detectable once a `CLLocation` fix with `sourceInformation` has been delivered |
| `isVpnConnected()` | ✅ | ✅ | Interface/transport scan — no VPN entitlement required |
| `isProxyEnabled()` | ✅ | ✅ | |
| `isScreenRecording()` | heuristic | ✅ (accurate) | iOS has a direct API (`UIScreen.isCaptured`); Android does not |
| `isScreenCaptured()` | ✅ | ✅ | "was a screenshot just taken" — true for ~5s after a screenshot event |
| `hasOverlayPermission()` | ✅ | always `false` | No overlay permission model on iOS |
| `isHookDetected()` | ✅ | ✅ | Umbrella: Frida + (Android) Xposed/LSPosed/Substrate/Riru |
| `isFridaDetected()` | ✅ | ✅ | |
| `isXposedDetected()` | ✅ | always `false` | Android-only framework |
| `isLSPosedDetected()` | ✅ | always `false` | Android-only framework |
| `isTampered()` | ✅ | ✅ | Zero-config heuristic — see [Security Notes](SECURITY_NOTES.md) |
| `getAppSignatureHash()` | ✅ | ✅ | Returns `Promise<string>` |
| `verifyAppSignature(hash)` | ✅ | ✅ | Compares against a hash you pin at build time |
| `verifyApkIntegrity(sha256)` | ✅ | always `true` | APK is an Android concept |
| `isSslPinningSupported()` | always `true` | always `true` | Informational — see [pinning helpers](#ssl-pinning-helpers) |

## `scan()`

```ts
const report: SecurityReport = await Security.scan();
```

```ts
interface SecurityReport {
  platform: 'android' | 'ios';
  secure: boolean;       // riskLevel === 'LOW'
  riskLevel: 'LOW' | 'MEDIUM' | 'HIGH' | 'CRITICAL';
  score: number;         // 0–100
  checks: SecurityChecks; // every individual boolean check, flattened
  reasons: string[];     // human-readable explanation per triggered check
  timestamp: number;
}
```

`scan()` runs every native check once and reuses the results — prefer it over calling every `isX()` individually when you want the full picture (fewer JSI round-trips, one consistent snapshot).

## Risk engine

```ts
import { configureRiskEngine, resetRiskEngine, DEFAULT_RISK_WEIGHTS, DEFAULT_RISK_THRESHOLDS } from '@rs-native-kit/security';

Security.configureRiskEngine({
  weights: { frida: 80, root: 40 },      // merge-overrides specific weights
  thresholds: { medium: 20, high: 40, critical: 60 },
});

Security.resetRiskEngine(); // back to library defaults
```

The engine is pure TypeScript (`src/risk/`) — it consumes the raw `checks`/`reasons` from native `scan()` and computes `score`/`riskLevel`/`secure`. This means you can retune scoring per-environment (e.g. stricter thresholds in a banking app vs. a loyalty app) without a native rebuild. See [DEFAULT_RISK_WEIGHTS](../src/risk/weights.ts) for the full default table.

`signatureValid` and `sslPinningSupported` are the two checks where `true` is the *good* outcome — the engine accounts for this automatically (risk is contributed when they're `false`, not `true`).

## Event system

```ts
const unsubscribe = Security.addListener((event: SecurityEvent) => {
  console.log(event.type, event.message, event.timestamp);
});

// later
unsubscribe();
// or
Security.removeListener(listener);
Security.removeAllListeners();
```

```ts
type SecurityEventType =
  | 'ROOT_DETECTED' | 'JAILBREAK_DETECTED'
  | 'VPN_CONNECTED' | 'PROXY_ENABLED'
  | 'SCREENSHOT' | 'SCREEN_RECORDING'
  | 'HOOK_DETECTED' | 'DEBUGGER'
  | 'TAMPER' | 'SIGNATURE_INVALID';
```

Native monitoring starts on the first `addListener()` call and stops automatically once the last listener unsubscribes — you don't need to manage lifecycle manually. `SCREENSHOT`/`SCREEN_RECORDING`/`VPN_CONNECTED` (and more) are driven by real OS push notifications where the platform provides one; everything else is re-evaluated on a ~4s poll and only fires on a false→true transition (see [Security Notes](SECURITY_NOTES.md#event-system-push-vs-poll) for exactly which is which).

## SSL pinning helpers

This SDK does not intercept network traffic — RN networking is owned by your app (OkHttp on Android, `URLSession` on iOS). These are typed helpers for assembling the pin config you hand to your networking layer:

```ts
import { buildCertificatePin, isValidPinFormat, isValidPinSet, hasExpiredPins } from '@rs-native-kit/security';

const pin = buildCertificatePin('api.example.com', ['sha256/AAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAA='], Date.now() + 90 * 86_400_000);

if (hasExpiredPins([pin])) {
  // rotate before your pin set hard-fails all requests
}
```

## Types

All public types are exported from the package root: `SecurityReport`, `SecurityChecks`, `SecurityCheckKey`, `RiskLevel`, `SecurityEvent`, `SecurityEventType`, `SecurityEventListener`, `RiskWeights`, `RiskThresholds`, `RiskEngineConfig`, `CertificatePin`, `SecurityUnsupportedPlatformError`.

```

`docs/BEST_PRACTICES.md`:

```md
# Best Practices

## Treat this as one signal, not the verdict

Send `scan()`'s `riskLevel`/`score`/`reasons` to your backend and combine it with server-side signals (device attestation, behavioral/fraud scoring, rate limiting). Never make a client-only "if rooted, block" decision for anything security-critical — the check itself runs on the same (potentially compromised) device it's evaluating. See [Security Notes](SECURITY_NOTES.md).

## Scan at the right moments, not on every render

- **App launch** (before showing sensitive content) and **before high-value actions** (login, payment, changing security settings) are the right times to call `scan()`.
- Don't call `scan()` in a render loop or on every navigation — cache the result for the session and re-scan on an interval (e.g. every few minutes) or in response to app-foreground events, combined with `addListener()` for real-time state changes in between.

```ts
useEffect(() => {
  const subscription = AppState.addEventListener('change', (state) => {
    if (state === 'active') void refreshScan();
  });
  return () => subscription.remove();
}, []);
```

## Use `addListener()` for state changes, `scan()` for point-in-time decisions

The event system exists so you don't have to poll `scan()` yourself to catch a screenshot, a newly-attached debugger, or a VPN connecting mid-session. Use it to react in real time (e.g. blur a sensitive screen on `SCREENSHOT`), and reserve `scan()` for decisions gated on a fresh, complete snapshot (e.g. right before submitting a payment).

## Pin your app signature at build time

```ts
const EXPECTED_SIGNATURE_HASH = process.env.EXPECTED_SIGNATURE_HASH; // injected at build time, per environment
const genuine = await Security.verifyAppSignature(EXPECTED_SIGNATURE_HASH);
```

Don't hardcode the hash in a way that's trivially greppable and swappable in a repackaged build — inject it per build environment (CI secret → build-time constant) so a naive "search and replace the expected hash" attack doesn't work against a casually inspected bundle.

## Tune risk weights to your actual policy, don't ship the defaults blindly

The default weights (`src/risk/weights.ts`) are a reasonable starting point, not a regulatory requirement. A banking app handling wire transfers should likely weight `frida`/`hookDetected`/`tampered` far more heavily than the defaults; a casual mobile game might not care about root/jailbreak at all. Decide your policy, then call `configureRiskEngine()` once at app startup.

## Don't over-react to `MEDIUM`

A rooted developer's own test device, a legitimate VPN app, or a corporate MDM-managed phone can all trigger individual `MEDIUM`-weighted checks without being a threat. Reserve hard blocks for `CRITICAL`, and use `HIGH`/`MEDIUM` for step-up auth or backend flagging rather than an outright deny — see [Examples](EXAMPLES.md#risk-based-step-up-authentication).

## Combine with `FLAG_SECURE` (Android) / disable screenshots on sensitive screens

`isScreenRecording()`/`isScreenCaptured()` are *detection*, not *prevention*. Pair them with `WindowManager.LayoutParams.FLAG_SECURE` (Android) on screens showing card numbers, balances, or credentials — that actually blocks capture, rather than just reporting it after the fact. iOS has no equivalent "block capture" API; detection + reactive blurring (on `SCREENSHOT`/`SCREEN_RECORDING` events) is the best available mitigation there.

## Keep the library updated

Detection techniques are inherently a moving target — root/jailbreak tooling evolves, and so should the signals this SDK checks for. Track releases and update regularly rather than pinning indefinitely; see the [Migration Guide](MIGRATION.md) for the versioning policy.

## Test your actual policy, not just that the SDK compiles

Unit-test your *risk policy* (the code that reads `SecurityReport` and decides what to do) with mocked reports covering every `riskLevel` and representative `checks` combinations — this is cheap, deterministic, and catches policy bugs (e.g. an inverted `secure` check) that no amount of real-device testing of the SDK itself will catch.

```

`docs/EXAMPLES.md`:

```md
# Examples

## Gate login behind a security scan

```ts
import Security from '@rs-native-kit/security';

async function canProceedToLogin(): Promise<boolean> {
  const report = await Security.scan();

  if (report.riskLevel === 'CRITICAL') {
    // e.g. Frida attached, or the app was tampered with — hard block.
    return false;
  }
  if (report.riskLevel === 'HIGH') {
    // e.g. rooted/jailbroken — allow, but flag the session server-side
    // for step-up auth or reduced transaction limits.
  }
  return true;
}
```

## Risk-based step-up authentication

```ts
const report = await Security.scan();

switch (report.riskLevel) {
  case 'LOW':
    return startBiometricLogin();
  case 'MEDIUM':
    return startBiometricLogin({ requireDeviceAttestation: true });
  case 'HIGH':
  case 'CRITICAL':
    return requireStepUpAuth(); // e.g. SMS OTP + fraud review
}
```

Send `report` (or at least `riskLevel`/`score`/`reasons`) to your backend alongside the login request — a client-reported risk level should inform server-side policy, never be trusted as the sole gate (see [Security Notes](SECURITY_NOTES.md)).

## React hook wrapping `scan()`

```tsx
import { useEffect, useState } from 'react';
import Security, { type SecurityReport } from '@rs-native-kit/security';

export function useSecurityScan() {
  const [report, setReport] = useState<SecurityReport | null>(null);
  const [error, setError] = useState<Error | null>(null);

  useEffect(() => {
    let cancelled = false;
    Security.scan()
      .then((result) => !cancelled && setReport(result))
      .catch((err) => !cancelled && setError(err));
    return () => {
      cancelled = true;
    };
  }, []);

  return { report, error };
}
```

## Subscribing to live events

```ts
useEffect(() => {
  return Security.addListener((event) => {
    if (event.type === 'SCREENSHOT') {
      analytics.track('sensitive_screen_screenshotted');
    }
    if (event.type === 'DEBUGGER' || event.type === 'HOOK_DETECTED') {
      forceLogout();
    }
  });
}, []);
```

## Pinning a known-good app signature

Fetch the hash once (e.g. print it in a debug build), then pin it as an app-side constant:

```ts
// One-time, in a debug build:
console.log(await Security.getAppSignatureHash());

// In production code:
const EXPECTED_SIGNATURE_HASH = 'your-hash-here';
const isGenuine = await Security.verifyAppSignature(EXPECTED_SIGNATURE_HASH);
```

## Custom risk weighting for a fintech app

```ts
Security.configureRiskEngine({
  weights: {
    root: 60,          // heavier than the default 30
    jailbreak: 60,
    frida: 100,         // any hooking framework is an instant CRITICAL
    hookDetected: 100,
  },
  thresholds: { medium: 15, high: 30, critical: 50 }, // stricter bands
});
```

## Running the example app

```sh
git clone <this repo>
cd security
yarn install

# Android (device or emulator)
yarn example android

# iOS (simulator or device)
yarn example ios

# Web preview of the dashboard UI only — see FAQ for why detections don't run there
yarn example web
```

The example app (`example/src/App.tsx`) is a working dashboard: security score, per-check status grid, live event log, and a refresh button.

```

`docs/FAQ.md`:

```md
# FAQ

### Why Nitro Modules instead of TurboModules?

Nitro is JSI-based and New-Architecture-only: synchronous-capable calls, strong TypeScript-to-native type generation, and no bridge serialization overhead — meaningfully faster for a module that may run a dozen small checks per `scan()` call. For a module with no native UI component, it's a strict upgrade over hand-written TurboModule codegen; Fabric (the renderer) doesn't apply here either way, since there's nothing to render. See [Architecture](../README.md#architecture).

### Does this work on the Old Architecture?

No. Nitro Modules requires the New Architecture. [Migrate to the New Architecture](https://reactnative.dev/docs/new-architecture-intro) first — it's been the RN default since 0.76.

### Can I run this in the web preview?

The example app's `yarn example web` target exists to preview the **dashboard UI** with `react-native-web`/Vite — it is not a supported detection target. Every detection method throws `SecurityUnsupportedPlatformError` on web rather than pretending the device is secure (see [Security Notes](SECURITY_NOTES.md#web-unsupported-platforms)). Only Android and iOS are real targets.

### Why does `isTampered()` return `true` on my debug build?

That's correct/expected — `isTampered()`'s zero-config heuristic includes "this is a debug/development-signed build" (Android: `FLAG_DEBUGGABLE`; iOS: `get-task-allow` entitlement in the provisioning profile), which is true for every local dev build by design. It should read `false` on a properly signed release/App Store build. If you need a stronger, non-heuristic check, use `verifyAppSignature()`/`verifyApkIntegrity()` with a hash you pin at build time.

### Why is `isRooted()` always `false` on iOS (and `isJailbroken()` always `false` on Android)?

Root and jailbreak are platform-specific concepts. Rather than mapping one onto the other (which every "cross-platform" library that exposes a single `isCompromised()` boolean effectively does, hiding which check actually ran), this SDK keeps them honest and separate. Use `scan()`'s `checks.root`/`checks.jailbreak` if you want a single object to inspect regardless of platform.

### How accurate is emulator detection?

100% on iOS (`targetEnvironment(simulator)` is a compile-time flag baked into the correct binary slice — there's no way for it to be wrong). On Android, it's signal-based (build fingerprint, known emulator packages, QEMU artifacts) and can theoretically be spoofed by a sufficiently customized emulator build, though this covers the common ones (stock AVD, Genymotion, BlueStacks, Nox, MEmu, LDPlayer) reliably.

### Does this intercept my network requests for SSL pinning?

No. `ssl/` ships typed helpers for building/validating a pin set (`buildCertificatePin`, `isValidPinSet`, `hasExpiredPins`) — you still enforce pinning in your networking layer (OkHttp `CertificatePinner` on Android, `URLSessionDelegate` trust evaluation on iOS). See [API Reference](API.md#ssl-pinning-helpers).

### Can I customize which checks contribute to the risk score?

Yes — `Security.configureRiskEngine({ weights, thresholds })`. The engine runs entirely in JS, so this doesn't require a native rebuild. See [Examples](EXAMPLES.md#custom-risk-weighting-for-a-fintech-app).

### Will this get my app rejected from the App Store / Play Store?

No API used here is private or against store policy — root/jailbreak/hook detection, network interface introspection, and provisioning-profile inspection are all standard techniques used by shipped banking/fintech apps. The one behavior worth knowing about: the jailbreak `fork()`/spawn probe (iOS) briefly spawns and immediately terminates a child process as a sandbox-restriction signal — this uses `posix_spawn`, not the raw (Swift-unavailable) `fork()` syscall, and is a well-established technique in shipped App Store apps.

### Does this replace Play Integrity API / App Attest?

No — it complements them. See [Security Notes](SECURITY_NOTES.md#threat-model) for why client-side detection alone can't provide the same guarantee as OS/hardware-anchored attestation, and why you should use both.

```

`docs/INSTALLATION.md`:

```md
# Installation

## Requirements

| | Minimum |
|---|---|
| React Native | 0.72 (New Architecture only — see [FAQ](FAQ.md#does-this-work-on-the-old-architecture)) |
| Android | API 24 (Android 7.0) |
| iOS | 15.1 |
| `react-native-nitro-modules` | ^0.36 |

## 1. Install the package

```sh
npm install @rs-native-kit/security react-native-nitro-modules
# or
yarn add @rs-native-kit/security react-native-nitro-modules
```

`react-native-nitro-modules` is a required **peer dependency** — this library's native module is built on it.

## 2. iOS

```sh
cd ios && pod install
```

No manual Info.plist changes are required for the core detection APIs. Two optional entries improve accuracy:

- **Cydia URL scheme detection** (one of several jailbreak signals): add `cydia` to `LSApplicationQueriesSchemes` in `Info.plist`. Without it, `canOpenURL` for `cydia://` always returns `false` (iOS 9+ policy), so this specific signal is silently skipped — the other jailbreak checks (file scan, sandbox-escape test, dylib inspection, process-spawn probe) still run.

  ```xml
  <key>LSApplicationQueriesSchemes</key>
  <array>
    <string>cydia</string>
  </array>
  ```

## 3. Android

Autolinking handles everything — no manual `MainApplication`/Gradle edits needed. The library declares two permissions in its own manifest, merged automatically into your app:

- `android.permission.INTERNET` — used only for a local loopback socket probe against Frida's default ports (`isFridaDetected`/`isHookDetected`). Nearly every RN app already has this permission for networking.
- `android.permission.ACCESS_NETWORK_STATE` — used for VPN/proxy transport checks.

### ProGuard / R8

If you enable code shrinking, no extra rules are needed for consumers — the native Kotlin classes are internal to the library and accessed only through the generated Nitro spec, which already ships the correct `@Keep`/`@DoNotStrip` annotations.

## 4. Verify

```ts
import Security from '@rs-native-kit/security';

Security.scan().then(console.log);
```

Run on a real device or emulator/simulator (see [FAQ](FAQ.md#can-i-run-this-in-the-web-preview) for why this doesn't work in the example app's web/vite preview).

## Old Architecture / bridge apps

Not supported. Nitro Modules requires the New Architecture (JSI). If you're on the old architecture, [migrate to the New Architecture](https://reactnative.dev/docs/new-architecture-intro) first — it's been the RN default since 0.76.

```

`docs/MIGRATION.md`:

```md
# Migration Guide

## From other security libraries

### `jail-monkey`

| jail-monkey | @rs-native-kit/security |
|---|---|
| `JailMonkey.isJailBroken()` | `await Security.isRooted()` (Android) / `await Security.isJailbroken()` (iOS) |
| `JailMonkey.canMockLocation()` | `await Security.isMockLocationEnabled()` |
| `JailMonkey.trustFall()` | `await Security.isTampered()` |
| `JailMonkey.isOnExternalStorage()` | not covered — out of scope |
| `JailMonkey.hookDetected()` | `await Security.isHookDetected()` |

Key difference: jail-monkey's `isJailBroken()` is a single cross-platform boolean; this SDK splits root (Android) and jailbreak (iOS) into distinct, platform-honest methods so you're never asking an Android device "is this jailbroken."

### `react-native-root-detection` / `react-native-device-info` (root/emulator bits)

Both are single-purpose; this SDK is a superset. Swap direct calls 1:1:

```diff
- import { isRootedExperimentalAsync } from 'react-native-root-detection';
- const rooted = await isRootedExperimentalAsync();
+ import Security from '@rs-native-kit/security';
+ const rooted = await Security.isRooted();
```

```diff
- import DeviceInfo from 'react-native-device-info';
- const isEmulator = await DeviceInfo.isEmulator();
+ import Security from '@rs-native-kit/security';
+ const isEmulator = await Security.isEmulator();
```

You can run this SDK alongside either during a trial migration — they don't conflict (no shared native module names, no shared JS globals).

## Version policy

This package follows semantic versioning:

- **Patch** (`0.1.x`): bug fixes, detection accuracy improvements, doc updates. No API changes.
- **Minor** (`0.x.0`): new checks, new event types, new optional config — always additive, never breaking.
- **Major** (`x.0.0`): reserved for breaking changes (renamed methods, changed `SecurityReport` shape, changed default risk weights in a way that shifts existing `riskLevel` classifications). A dedicated "Migrating to vX" section will be added here for every major release, with a diff-style before/after for every breaking change.

Until `1.0.0`, treat the public API as stable but not yet permanently frozen — default risk *weights* (not the API shape) may be tuned based on real-world false-positive/negative reports.

```

`docs/SECURITY_NOTES.md`:

```md
# Security Notes

Read this before shipping to a banking, fintech, healthcare, or government app. **No on-device SDK — this one included — is a substitute for server-side verification.** Anything running inside the app process can, in principle, be inspected or patched by an attacker who controls the device. This SDK raises the cost of tampering and gives you real, actionable signal; it does not make bypass impossible.

## Threat model

This SDK is designed to detect **opportunistic and semi-sophisticated** threats:

- A user's own rooted/jailbroken device (most fraud rings run on cheap, rooted farms of real or emulated devices).
- Off-the-shelf instrumentation (Frida, Xposed/LSPosed) used to automate credential stuffing, coupon abuse, or replay attacks.
- Basic repackaging (a modified APK/IPA redistributed through a third-party store).
- Casual observation (VPN/proxy MITM setups, screen recording of sensitive screens).

It is **not** designed to stop a well-resourced, targeted attacker who statically patches the app binary to always return "not detected" — no client-side check can, because the attacker controls the exact process running the check. For that threat class, use:

- **Server-side attestation**: [Android Play Integrity API](https://developer.android.com/google/play/integrity) and [Apple App Attest](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/devicecheck)/DeviceCheck. These are cryptographically anchored to the OS/hardware and cannot be spoofed by patching your app — they're the correct tool for a hard guarantee, and this SDK is complementary to them, not a replacement.
- **Certificate/public-key pinning** enforced in your networking layer (this SDK's `ssl/` helpers assist with building the pin config, but the actual enforcement must live in OkHttp/`URLSession`).
- **Server-side risk scoring** that combines this SDK's client-reported signal with request-pattern anomaly detection, device fingerprinting, and behavioral signals you can't get from any client SDK.

## What "zero-config" checks can and can't tell you

- `scan()`'s `signatureValid` is always `true` in the default, zero-config call — there is no "known good" hash to compare against without you supplying one. **Call `verifyAppSignature(expectedHash)` with a hash you pin at build time for an authoritative signature check.**
- `isTampered()` is a heuristic (debuggable/development-signed build detection), not a cryptographic guarantee. It will not catch a competently repackaged app re-signed with a valid certificate — see [Integrity checks](#integrity-checks) below.

## Integrity checks

- **Android** reads the real signing certificate via `PackageManager` (`GET_SIGNING_CERTIFICATES`), so `getAppSignatureHash()`/`verifyAppSignature()` are a genuine cryptographic fingerprint of the installed APK's signer.
- **iOS has no equivalent runtime API.** Apple's Code Signing Services (`SecStaticCodeCheckValidity` and friends) are **macOS-only** — they don't exist in the iOS SDK at all, and there's no other public API to read your own signing certificate from inside a running iOS app (by design — the OS enforces code signing before your code ever executes, so it doesn't need to expose a re-verification API to you). This SDK's iOS `getAppSignatureHash()` hashes the embedded provisioning profile (Development/Ad Hoc/Enterprise builds) or the bundle's `CodeResources` manifest (App Store builds) as a practical fingerprint of "did this exact build's signing identity change" — it is **not** cryptographic proof of signature validity the way the Android check is. For a hard iOS integrity guarantee, use App Attest.

## Event system: push vs. poll

Real OS push sources (accurate, event-driven, no polling latency):

| Event | Android | iOS |
|---|---|---|
| `SCREENSHOT` | `MediaStore` `ContentObserver` | `UIApplication.userDidTakeScreenshotNotification` |
| `SCREEN_RECORDING` | — (polled) | `UIScreen.capturedDidChangeNotification` |
| `VPN_CONNECTED` / `PROXY_ENABLED` | `ConnectivityManager.NetworkCallback` | — (polled) |

Everything else (`ROOT_DETECTED`, `JAILBREAK_DETECTED`, `HOOK_DETECTED`, `DEBUGGER`, `TAMPER`) is re-evaluated on a ~4-second poll and only emitted on a false→true transition, because Android/iOS don't expose a push API for "root state changed" or "a debugger just attached." A 4-second detection latency is the tradeoff for not running detection logic continuously on a tight loop (battery/CPU).

## Platform asymmetry is intentional, not a bug

Several checks are meaningful on one platform and hard-coded `false`/`true` on the other (`isRooted`/`isJailbroken`, `hasOverlayPermission`, `isXposedDetected`, etc. — see the [API reference](API.md) table). This SDK never fabricates a plausible-looking answer for a concept that doesn't exist on a platform; it reports the honest "not applicable" value instead of guessing.

## Web/unsupported platforms

If this package is somehow loaded on a platform Nitro doesn't support (e.g. bundled for `react-native-web`), every detection method **throws `SecurityUnsupportedPlatformError`** rather than silently returning `false`/"secure." A security SDK reporting a false negative is worse than it crashing loudly. Handle this explicitly if your app has a web target (see the example app's error-state UI for a pattern).

## Verification status of this implementation

Everything in this SDK compiles and links into a real, running app on both platforms (verified via full Android APK builds and full iOS simulator + device builds during development). What has **not** been empirically validated in this environment: detection *accuracy* against an actual rooted/jailbroken device, an actual Frida-attached process, or an actual repackaged/tampered build — that requires physical compromised test devices and a live pentest, which is outside what a development sandbox can provide. **Before shipping to production, especially for a regulated app, commission a penetration test against real compromised devices and Frida/Xposed/LSPosed-instrumented processes.**

```

`docs/TROUBLESHOOTING.md`:

```md
# Troubleshooting

## `SecurityUnsupportedPlatformError: "..." is not supported on this platform`

You're calling a detection method on a platform other than Android/iOS (most commonly: the `react-native-web`/Vite preview target). This is intentional — see [Security Notes](SECURITY_NOTES.md#web-unsupported-platforms). Guard the call:

```ts
import { Platform } from 'react-native';

if (Platform.OS === 'android' || Platform.OS === 'ios') {
  const report = await Security.scan();
}
```

## Build fails with "No such module 'NitroModules'" (iOS)

Run `pod install` after installing/upgrading the package — Nitro's generated Swift/C++ bridge files are wired through CocoaPods and won't exist until pods are installed:

```sh
cd ios && pod install
```

If you recently pulled changes that touched `src/Security.nitro.ts`, also regenerate the native glue:

```sh
yarn nitrogen
cd ios && pod install
```

## `NitroModules.createHybridObject` throws / native module not found at runtime

Almost always a stale build. Nitro's native glue is generated at build time, not runtime:

1. `yarn nitrogen` (regenerates `nitrogen/generated/`)
2. iOS: `cd ios && pod install`, then a clean rebuild (`Product > Clean Build Folder` in Xcode, or delete `ios/build`)
3. Android: `cd android && ./gradlew clean` (or delete `android/.cxx` and `android/build`), then rebuild

## Android build fails referencing `RsNativeKitSecurity` / `Security` C++ symbols

Make sure `yarn nitrogen` has been run after any change to `src/Security.nitro.ts` or `nitro.json`, and that you're not mixing a stale `nitrogen/generated/` directory from before an `iosModuleName`/`androidNamespace` change. Delete `nitrogen/generated/` and re-run `yarn nitrogen` if in doubt.

## `isFridaDetected()` / hook checks seem slow

The Frida port-scan check opens a loopback socket with a short (200ms) timeout per port — on a device with a strict firewall or unusual network stack, this can add up to ~400ms across both default Frida ports. This runs off the JS thread already (`Promise.parallel`), so it shouldn't cause UI jank, but if you're calling `scan()` synchronously before rendering a screen, show a loading state rather than blocking navigation.

## Screenshot/screen-recording events never fire

- **Android**: the `MediaStore` `ContentObserver` requires the OS to actually write a new row to `MediaStore.Images.Media` — some custom ROMs or third-party screenshot tools that bypass the standard screenshot path won't trigger it.
- **iOS**: `UIScreen.capturedDidChangeNotification`/`userDidTakeScreenshotNotification` only fire while your app is in the foreground, per Apple's documented behavior.
- Both: confirm you actually have an active listener — `addListener()` must be called (and its returned unsubscribe function *not* called) for native monitoring to be running at all; see [API Reference](API.md#event-system).

## `verifyAppSignature()` / `getAppSignatureHash()` throws on iOS

This is expected if the running bundle has neither an embedded provisioning profile nor a `_CodeSignature/CodeResources` file — e.g. certain ad-hoc test harnesses. On a normal Development, Ad Hoc, Enterprise, or App Store build, one of the two is always present. See [Security Notes](SECURITY_NOTES.md#integrity-checks) for why iOS signature verification works differently from Android's.

## Jest tests fail with "Cannot find module '@rs-native-kit/security'" in a consuming app

Make sure your Jest config's `moduleFileExtensions`/`transformIgnorePatterns` follow the standard React Native preset (`@react-native/jest-preset`), and that you're not accidentally resolving the package's TypeScript source instead of `lib/` outside of this repo's own test suite (the `rs-native-kit-security-source` custom export condition is only meant to be used inside this repo).

## Still stuck?

Open an issue with: RN version, `react-native-nitro-modules` version, platform + OS version, and the exact error/stack trace. See [CONTRIBUTING.md](../CONTRIBUTING.md).

```

`example/README.md`:

```md
This is a new [**React Native**](https://reactnative.dev) project, bootstrapped using [`@react-native-community/cli`](https://github.com/react-native-community/cli).

# Getting Started

> **Note**: Make sure you have completed the [Set Up Your Environment](https://reactnative.dev/docs/set-up-your-environment) guide before proceeding.

## Step 1: Start Metro

First, you will need to run **Metro**, the JavaScript build tool for React Native.

To start the Metro dev server, run the following command from the root of your React Native project:

```sh
# Using npm
npm start

# OR using Yarn
yarn start
```

## Step 2: Build and run your app

With Metro running, open a new terminal window/pane from the root of your React Native project, and use one of the following commands to build and run your Android or iOS app:

### Android

```sh
# Using npm
npm run android

# OR using Yarn
yarn android
```

### iOS

For iOS, remember to install CocoaPods dependencies (this only needs to be run on first clone or after updating native deps).

The first time you create a new project, run the Ruby bundler to install CocoaPods itself:

```sh
bundle install
```

Then, and every time you update your native dependencies, run:

```sh
bundle exec pod install
```

For more information, please visit [CocoaPods Getting Started guide](https://guides.cocoapods.org/using/getting-started.html).

```sh
# Using npm
npm run ios

# OR using Yarn
yarn ios
```

If everything is set up correctly, you should see your new app running in the Android Emulator, iOS Simulator, or your connected device.

This is one way to run your app — you can also build it directly from Android Studio or Xcode.

## Step 3: Modify your app

Now that you have successfully run the app, let's make changes!

Open `App.tsx` in your text editor of choice and make some changes. When you save, your app will automatically update and reflect these changes — this is powered by [Fast Refresh](https://reactnative.dev/docs/fast-refresh).

When you want to forcefully reload, for example to reset the state of your app, you can perform a full reload:

- **Android**: Press the <kbd>R</kbd> key twice or select **"Reload"** from the **Dev Menu**, accessed via <kbd>Ctrl</kbd> + <kbd>M</kbd> (Windows/Linux) or <kbd>Cmd ⌘</kbd> + <kbd>M</kbd> (macOS).
- **iOS**: Press <kbd>R</kbd> in iOS Simulator.

## Congratulations! :tada:

You've successfully run and modified your React Native App. :partying_face:

- If you're curious to learn more about React Native, check out the [docs](https://reactnative.dev/docs/getting-started).

package rsnativekit.security.example

import com.facebook.react.ReactActivity
import com.facebook.react.ReactActivityDelegate
import com.facebook.react.defaults.DefaultNewArchitectureEntryPoint.fabricEnabled
import com.facebook.react.defaults.DefaultReactActivityDelegate

class MainActivity : ReactActivity() {

  /**
   * Returns the name of the main component registered from JavaScript. This is used to schedule
   * rendering of the component.
   */
  override fun getMainComponentName(): String = "SecurityExample"

  /**
   * Returns the instance of the [ReactActivityDelegate]. We use [DefaultReactActivityDelegate]
   * which allows you to enable New Architecture with a single boolean flags [fabricEnabled]
   */
  override fun createReactActivityDelegate(): ReactActivityDelegate =
      DefaultReactActivityDelegate(this, mainComponentName, fabricEnabled)
}

package rsnativekit.security.example

import android.app.Application
import com.facebook.react.PackageList
import com.facebook.react.ReactApplication
import com.facebook.react.ReactHost
import com.facebook.react.ReactNativeApplicationEntryPoint.loadReactNative
import com.facebook.react.defaults.DefaultReactHost.getDefaultReactHost

class MainApplication : Application(), ReactApplication {

  override val reactHost: ReactHost by lazy {
    getDefaultReactHost(
      context = applicationContext,
      packageList =
        PackageList(this).packages.apply {
          // Packages that cannot be autolinked yet can be added manually here, for example:
          // add(MyReactNativePackage())
        },
    )
  }

  override fun onCreate() {
    super.onCreate()
    loadReactNative(this)
  }
}

`example/android/gradlew`:

```
#!/bin/sh

#
# Copyright © 2015 the original authors.
#
# Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the "License");
# you may not use this file except in compliance with the License.
# You may obtain a copy of the License at
#
#      https://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0
#
# Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software
# distributed under the License is distributed on an "AS IS" BASIS,
# WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.
# See the License for the specific language governing permissions and
# limitations under the License.
#
# SPDX-License-Identifier: Apache-2.0
#

##############################################################################
#
#   Gradle start up script for POSIX generated by Gradle.
#
#   Important for running:
#
#   (1) You need a POSIX-compliant shell to run this script. If your /bin/sh is
#       noncompliant, but you have some other compliant shell such as ksh or
#       bash, then to run this script, type that shell name before the whole
#       command line, like:
#
#           ksh Gradle
#
#       Busybox and similar reduced shells will NOT work, because this script
#       requires all of these POSIX shell features:
#         * functions;
#         * expansions «$var», «${var}», «${var:-default}», «${var+SET}»,
#           «${var#prefix}», «${var%suffix}», and «$( cmd )»;
#         * compound commands having a testable exit status, especially «case»;
#         * various built-in commands including «command», «set», and «ulimit».
#
#   Important for patching:
#
#   (2) This script targets any POSIX shell, so it avoids extensions provided
#       by Bash, Ksh, etc; in particular arrays are avoided.
#
#       The "traditional" practice of packing multiple parameters into a
#       space-separated string is a well documented source of bugs and security
#       problems, so this is (mostly) avoided, by progressively accumulating
#       options in "$@", and eventually passing that to Java.
#
#       Where the inherited environment variables (DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS, JAVA_OPTS,
#       and GRADLE_OPTS) rely on word-splitting, this is performed explicitly;
#       see the in-line comments for details.
#
#       There are tweaks for specific operating systems such as AIX, CygWin,
#       Darwin, MinGW, and NonStop.
#
#   (3) This script is generated from the Groovy template
#       https://github.com/gradle/gradle/blob/HEAD/platforms/jvm/plugins-application/src/main/resources/org/gradle/api/internal/plugins/unixStartScript.txt
#       within the Gradle project.
#
#       You can find Gradle at https://github.com/gradle/gradle/.
#
##############################################################################

# Attempt to set APP_HOME

# Resolve links: $0 may be a link
app_path=$0

# Need this for daisy-chained symlinks.
while
    APP_HOME=${app_path%"${app_path##*/}"}  # leaves a trailing /; empty if no leading path
    [ -h "$app_path" ]
do
    ls=$( ls -ld "$app_path" )
    link=${ls#*' -> '}
    case $link in             #(
      /*)   app_path=$link ;; #(
      *)    app_path=$APP_HOME$link ;;
    esac
done

# This is normally unused
# shellcheck disable=SC2034
APP_BASE_NAME=${0##*/}
# Discard cd standard output in case $CDPATH is set (https://github.com/gradle/gradle/issues/25036)
APP_HOME=$( cd -P "${APP_HOME:-./}" > /dev/null && printf '%s\n' "$PWD" ) || exit

# Use the maximum available, or set MAX_FD != -1 to use that value.
MAX_FD=maximum

warn () {
    echo "$*"
} >&2

die () {
    echo
    echo "$*"
    echo
    exit 1
} >&2

# OS specific support (must be 'true' or 'false').
cygwin=false
msys=false
darwin=false
nonstop=false
case "$( uname )" in                #(
  CYGWIN* )         cygwin=true  ;; #(
  Darwin* )         darwin=true  ;; #(
  MSYS* | MINGW* )  msys=true    ;; #(
  NONSTOP* )        nonstop=true ;;
esac



# Determine the Java command to use to start the JVM.
if [ -n "$JAVA_HOME" ] ; then
    if [ -x "$JAVA_HOME/jre/sh/java" ] ; then
        # IBM's JDK on AIX uses strange locations for the executables
        JAVACMD=$JAVA_HOME/jre/sh/java
    else
        JAVACMD=$JAVA_HOME/bin/java
    fi
    if [ ! -x "$JAVACMD" ] ; then
        die "ERROR: JAVA_HOME is set to an invalid directory: $JAVA_HOME

Please set the JAVA_HOME variable in your environment to match the
location of your Java installation."
    fi
else
    JAVACMD=java
    if ! command -v java >/dev/null 2>&1
    then
        die "ERROR: JAVA_HOME is not set and no 'java' command could be found in your PATH.

Please set the JAVA_HOME variable in your environment to match the
location of your Java installation."
    fi
fi

# Increase the maximum file descriptors if we can.
if ! "$cygwin" && ! "$darwin" && ! "$nonstop" ; then
    case $MAX_FD in #(
      max*)
        # In POSIX sh, ulimit -H is undefined. That's why the result is checked to see if it worked.
        # shellcheck disable=SC2039,SC3045
        MAX_FD=$( ulimit -H -n ) ||
            warn "Could not query maximum file descriptor limit"
    esac
    case $MAX_FD in  #(
      '' | soft) :;; #(
      *)
        # In POSIX sh, ulimit -n is undefined. That's why the result is checked to see if it worked.
        # shellcheck disable=SC2039,SC3045
        ulimit -n "$MAX_FD" ||
            warn "Could not set maximum file descriptor limit to $MAX_FD"
    esac
fi

# Collect all arguments for the java command, stacking in reverse order:
#   * args from the command line
#   * the main class name
#   * -classpath
#   * -D...appname settings
#   * --module-path (only if needed)
#   * DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS, JAVA_OPTS, and GRADLE_OPTS environment variables.

# For Cygwin or MSYS, switch paths to Windows format before running java
if "$cygwin" || "$msys" ; then
    APP_HOME=$( cygpath --path --mixed "$APP_HOME" )

    JAVACMD=$( cygpath --unix "$JAVACMD" )

    # Now convert the arguments - kludge to limit ourselves to /bin/sh
    for arg do
        if
            case $arg in                                #(
              -*)   false ;;                            # don't mess with options #(
              /?*)  t=${arg#/} t=/${t%%/*}              # looks like a POSIX filepath
                    [ -e "$t" ] ;;                      #(
              *)    false ;;
            esac
        then
            arg=$( cygpath --path --ignore --mixed "$arg" )
        fi
        # Roll the args list around exactly as many times as the number of
        # args, so each arg winds up back in the position where it started, but
        # possibly modified.
        #
        # NB: a `for` loop captures its iteration list before it begins, so
        # changing the positional parameters here affects neither the number of
        # iterations, nor the values presented in `arg`.
        shift                   # remove old arg
        set -- "$@" "$arg"      # push replacement arg
    done
fi


# Add default JVM options here. You can also use JAVA_OPTS and GRADLE_OPTS to pass JVM options to this script.
DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS='"-Xmx64m" "-Xms64m"'

# Collect all arguments for the java command:
#   * DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS, JAVA_OPTS, and optsEnvironmentVar are not allowed to contain shell fragments,
#     and any embedded shellness will be escaped.
#   * For example: A user cannot expect ${Hostname} to be expanded, as it is an environment variable and will be
#     treated as '${Hostname}' itself on the command line.

set -- \
        "-Dorg.gradle.appname=$APP_BASE_NAME" \
        -jar "$APP_HOME/gradle/wrapper/gradle-wrapper.jar" \
        "$@"

# Stop when "xargs" is not available.
if ! command -v xargs >/dev/null 2>&1
then
    die "xargs is not available"
fi

# Use "xargs" to parse quoted args.
#
# With -n1 it outputs one arg per line, with the quotes and backslashes removed.
#
# In Bash we could simply go:
#
#   readarray ARGS < <( xargs -n1 <<<"$var" ) &&
#   set -- "${ARGS[@]}" "$@"
#
# but POSIX shell has neither arrays nor command substitution, so instead we
# post-process each arg (as a line of input to sed) to backslash-escape any
# character that might be a shell metacharacter, then use eval to reverse
# that process (while maintaining the separation between arguments), and wrap
# the whole thing up as a single "set" statement.
#
# This will of course break if any of these variables contains a newline or
# an unmatched quote.
#

eval "set -- $(
        printf '%s\n' "$DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS $JAVA_OPTS $GRADLE_OPTS" |
        xargs -n1 |
        sed ' s~[^-[:alnum:]+,./:=@_]~\\&~g; ' |
        tr '\n' ' '
    )" '"$@"'

exec "$JAVACMD" "$@"

```

`example/android/gradlew.bat`:

```bat
@REM Copyright (c) Meta Platforms, Inc. and affiliates.
@REM
@REM This source code is licensed under the MIT license found in the
@REM LICENSE file in the root directory of this source tree.

@rem
@rem Copyright 2015 the original author or authors.
@rem
@rem Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the "License");
@rem you may not use this file except in compliance with the License.
@rem You may obtain a copy of the License at
@rem
@rem      https://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0
@rem
@rem Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software
@rem distributed under the License is distributed on an "AS IS" BASIS,
@rem WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.
@rem See the License for the specific language governing permissions and
@rem limitations under the License.
@rem
@rem SPDX-License-Identifier: Apache-2.0
@rem

@if "%DEBUG%"=="" @echo off
@rem ##########################################################################
@rem
@rem  Gradle startup script for Windows
@rem
@rem ##########################################################################

@rem Set local scope for the variables with windows NT shell
if "%OS%"=="Windows_NT" setlocal

set DIRNAME=%~dp0
if "%DIRNAME%"=="" set DIRNAME=.
@rem This is normally unused
set APP_BASE_NAME=%~n0
set APP_HOME=%DIRNAME%

@rem Resolve any "." and ".." in APP_HOME to make it shorter.
for %%i in ("%APP_HOME%") do set APP_HOME=%%~fi

@rem Add default JVM options here. You can also use JAVA_OPTS and GRADLE_OPTS to pass JVM options to this script.
set DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS="-Xmx64m" "-Xms64m"

@rem Find java.exe
if defined JAVA_HOME goto findJavaFromJavaHome

set JAVA_EXE=java.exe
%JAVA_EXE% -version >NUL 2>&1
if %ERRORLEVEL% equ 0 goto execute

echo. 1>&2
echo ERROR: JAVA_HOME is not set and no 'java' command could be found in your PATH. 1>&2
echo. 1>&2
echo Please set the JAVA_HOME variable in your environment to match the 1>&2
echo location of your Java installation. 1>&2

goto fail

:findJavaFromJavaHome
set JAVA_HOME=%JAVA_HOME:"=%
set JAVA_EXE=%JAVA_HOME%/bin/java.exe

if exist "%JAVA_EXE%" goto execute

echo. 1>&2
echo ERROR: JAVA_HOME is set to an invalid directory: %JAVA_HOME% 1>&2
echo. 1>&2
echo Please set the JAVA_HOME variable in your environment to match the 1>&2
echo location of your Java installation. 1>&2

goto fail

:execute
@rem Setup the command line



@rem Execute Gradle
"%JAVA_EXE%" %DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS% %JAVA_OPTS% %GRADLE_OPTS% "-Dorg.gradle.appname=%APP_BASE_NAME%" -jar "%APP_HOME%\gradle\wrapper\gradle-wrapper.jar" %*

:end
@rem End local scope for the variables with windows NT shell
if %ERRORLEVEL% equ 0 goto mainEnd

:fail
rem Set variable GRADLE_EXIT_CONSOLE if you need the _script_ return code instead of
rem the _cmd.exe /c_ return code!
set EXIT_CODE=%ERRORLEVEL%
if %EXIT_CODE% equ 0 set EXIT_CODE=1
if not ""=="%GRADLE_EXIT_CONSOLE%" exit %EXIT_CODE%
exit /b %EXIT_CODE%

:mainEnd
if "%OS%"=="Windows_NT" endlocal

:omega

```

```js
import { AppRegistry } from 'react-native';
import App from './src/App';
import { name as appName } from './app.json';

AppRegistry.registerComponent(appName, () => App);

if (typeof document !== 'undefined') {
  AppRegistry.runApplication(appName, {
    rootTag: document.getElementById('root'),
  });
}

`example/ios/Podfile`:

```
# Resolve react_native_pods.rb with node to allow for hoisting
require Pod::Executable.execute_command('node', ['-p',
  'require.resolve(
    "react-native/scripts/react_native_pods.rb",
    {paths: [process.argv[1]]},
  )', __dir__]).strip

platform :ios, min_ios_version_supported
prepare_react_native_project!

linkage = ENV['USE_FRAMEWORKS']
if linkage != nil
  Pod::UI.puts "Configuring Pod with #{linkage}ally linked Frameworks".green
  use_frameworks! :linkage => linkage.to_sym
end

target 'SecurityExample' do
  config = use_native_modules!

  use_react_native!(
    :path => config[:reactNativePath],
    # An absolute path to your application root.
    :app_path => "#{Pod::Config.instance.installation_root}/.."
  )

  post_install do |installer|
    react_native_post_install(
      installer,
      config[:reactNativePath],
      :mac_catalyst_enabled => false,
      # :ccache_enabled => true
    )
  end
end

```

`example/ios/SecurityExample/AppDelegate.swift`:

```swift
import UIKit
import React
import React_RCTAppDelegate
import ReactAppDependencyProvider

@main
class AppDelegate: UIResponder, UIApplicationDelegate {
  var window: UIWindow?

  var reactNativeDelegate: ReactNativeDelegate?
  var reactNativeFactory: RCTReactNativeFactory?

  func application(
    _ application: UIApplication,
    didFinishLaunchingWithOptions launchOptions: [UIApplication.LaunchOptionsKey: Any]? = nil
  ) -> Bool {
    let delegate = ReactNativeDelegate()
    let factory = RCTReactNativeFactory(delegate: delegate)
    delegate.dependencyProvider = RCTAppDependencyProvider()

    reactNativeDelegate = delegate
    reactNativeFactory = factory

    window = UIWindow(frame: UIScreen.main.bounds)

    factory.startReactNative(
      withModuleName: "SecurityExample",
      in: window,
      launchOptions: launchOptions
    )

    return true
  }
}

class ReactNativeDelegate: RCTDefaultReactNativeFactoryDelegate {
  override func sourceURL(for bridge: RCTBridge) -> URL? {
    self.bundleURL()
  }

  override func bundleURL() -> URL? {
#if DEBUG
    RCTBundleURLProvider.sharedSettings().jsBundleURL(forBundleRoot: "index")
#else
    Bundle.main.url(forResource: "main", withExtension: "jsbundle")
#endif
  }
}

`example/src/App.tsx`:

```tsx
import { useCallback, useEffect, useState } from 'react';
import {
  ActivityIndicator,
  Pressable,
  ScrollView,
  StyleSheet,
  Text,
  View,
} from 'react-native';
import Security from '@rs-native-kit/security';
import type {
  RiskLevel,
  SecurityCheckKey,
  SecurityEvent,
  SecurityReport,
} from '@rs-native-kit/security';

const RISK_COLORS: Record<RiskLevel, string> = {
  LOW: '#16a34a',
  MEDIUM: '#d97706',
  HIGH: '#ea580c',
  CRITICAL: '#dc2626',
};

const CHECK_LABELS: Record<SecurityCheckKey, string> = {
  root: 'Root',
  jailbreak: 'Jailbreak',
  emulator: 'Emulator',
  developerMode: 'Developer mode',
  usbDebugging: 'USB debugging',
  vpn: 'VPN',
  proxy: 'Proxy',
  overlay: 'Overlay exposure',
  frida: 'Frida',
  xposed: 'Xposed',
  lsposed: 'LSPosed',
  magisk: 'Magisk',
  busybox: 'BusyBox',
  mockLocation: 'Mock location',
  hookDetected: 'Hook detected',
  tampered: 'Tampered',
  signatureValid: 'Signature valid',
  sslPinningSupported: 'SSL pinning supported',
  screenRecording: 'Screen recording',
  screenCaptured: 'Screen captured',
  debuggerAttached: 'Debugger attached',
};

/** Checks where `true` is the *good* outcome — everything else, `true` means risk. */
const POSITIVE_CHECKS = new Set<SecurityCheckKey>([
  'signatureValid',
  'sslPinningSupported',
]);

function isCheckHealthy(key: SecurityCheckKey, value: boolean): boolean {
  return POSITIVE_CHECKS.has(key) ? value : !value;
}

function formatTime(timestamp: number): string {
  return new Date(timestamp).toLocaleTimeString();
}

function ScoreCard({ report }: { report: SecurityReport }) {
  const color = RISK_COLORS[report.riskLevel];
  return (
    <View style={[styles.scoreCard, { borderColor: color }]}>
      <Text style={[styles.scoreValue, { color }]}>{report.score}</Text>
      <Text style={styles.scoreOutOf}>/ 100</Text>
      <View style={[styles.riskBadge, { backgroundColor: color }]}>
        <Text style={styles.riskBadgeText}>{report.riskLevel} RISK</Text>
      </View>
      <Text style={styles.secureLabel}>
        {report.secure ? '✅ Device is secure' : '⚠️ Device is not secure'}
      </Text>
      <Text style={styles.platformLabel}>
        Platform: {report.platform} · Scanned {formatTime(report.timestamp)}
      </Text>
    </View>
  );
}

function CheckRow({ label, healthy }: { label: string; healthy: boolean }) {
  return (
    <View style={styles.checkRow}>
      <Text style={styles.checkLabel}>{label}</Text>
      <View
        style={[
          styles.checkPill,
          { backgroundColor: healthy ? '#16a34a1a' : '#dc26261a' },
        ]}
      >
        <Text
          style={[
            styles.checkPillText,
            { color: healthy ? '#16a34a' : '#dc2626' },
          ]}
        >
          {healthy ? 'OK' : 'FLAGGED'}
        </Text>
      </View>
    </View>
  );
}

function EventRow({ event }: { event: SecurityEvent }) {
  return (
    <View style={styles.eventRow}>
      <Text style={styles.eventType}>{event.type}</Text>
      <Text style={styles.eventMessage}>{event.message}</Text>
      <Text style={styles.eventTime}>{formatTime(event.timestamp)}</Text>
    </View>
  );
}

export default function App() {
  const [report, setReport] = useState<SecurityReport | null>(null);
  const [loading, setLoading] = useState(true);
  const [error, setError] = useState<string | null>(null);
  const [events, setEvents] = useState<SecurityEvent[]>([]);

  const runScan = useCallback(() => {
    setLoading(true);
    setError(null);
    Security.scan()
      .then((result) => setReport(result))
      .catch((err: unknown) =>
        setError(err instanceof Error ? err.message : String(err))
      )
      .finally(() => setLoading(false));
  }, []);

  useEffect(() => {
    runScan();
  }, [runScan]);

  useEffect(() => {
    const unsubscribe = Security.addListener((event) => {
      setEvents((previous) => [event, ...previous].slice(0, 20));
    });
    return unsubscribe;
  }, []);

  return (
    <ScrollView style={styles.container} contentContainerStyle={styles.content}>
      <View style={styles.header}>
        <Text style={styles.title}>Security Dashboard</Text>
        <Pressable
          style={({ pressed }) => [
            styles.refreshButton,
            pressed && styles.refreshButtonPressed,
          ]}
          onPress={runScan}
          disabled={loading}
        >
          <Text style={styles.refreshButtonText}>
            {loading ? 'Scanning…' : 'Refresh'}
          </Text>
        </Pressable>
      </View>

      {loading && !report && (
        <ActivityIndicator style={styles.loadingIndicator} size="large" />
      )}

      {error && (
        <View style={styles.errorBox}>
          <Text style={styles.errorTitle}>Scan unavailable</Text>
          <Text style={styles.errorMessage}>{error}</Text>
        </View>
      )}

      {report && (
        <>
          <ScoreCard report={report} />

          <Text style={styles.sectionTitle}>Checks</Text>
          <View style={styles.checksCard}>
            {(Object.keys(report.checks) as SecurityCheckKey[]).map((key) => (
              <CheckRow
                key={key}
                label={CHECK_LABELS[key]}
                healthy={isCheckHealthy(key, report.checks[key])}
              />
            ))}
          </View>

          {report.reasons.length > 0 && (
            <>
              <Text style={styles.sectionTitle}>Reasons</Text>
              <View style={styles.reasonsCard}>
                {report.reasons.map((reason, index) => (
                  <Text key={`${reason}-${index}`} style={styles.reasonText}>
                    • {reason}
                  </Text>
                ))}
              </View>
            </>
          )}
        </>
      )}

      <Text style={styles.sectionTitle}>Live security events</Text>
      <View style={styles.eventsCard}>
        {events.length === 0 ? (
          <Text style={styles.emptyEventsText}>
            No events yet — this updates in real time as VPN/proxy state
            changes, screenshots are taken, or a threat is newly detected.
          </Text>
        ) : (
          events.map((event, index) => (
            <EventRow key={`${event.timestamp}-${index}`} event={event} />
          ))
        )}
      </View>
    </ScrollView>
  );
}

`ios/Detectors/DeveloperModeDetector.swift`:

```swift
import Foundation
#if canImport(CoreLocation)
import CoreLocation
#endif

final class DeveloperModeDetector {

  /// iOS has no public API for "Developer Mode" the way Android does.
  /// Best-effort proxy: an `embedded.mobileprovision` is bundled with
  /// development/ad-hoc/enterprise builds but never with App Store builds,
  /// so its presence is a reasonable (if imperfect) stand-in.
  func isDeveloperModeEnabled() -> Bool {
    guard let provisioningPath = Bundle.main.path(forResource: "embedded", ofType: "mobileprovision") else {
      return false
    }
    return FileManager.default.fileExists(atPath: provisioningPath)
  }

  /// USB debugging (ADB) has no iOS equivalent — Xcode uses a paired
  /// lockdown/trust relationship, not a toggleable device setting. Always
  /// `false`; documented as a platform gap rather than silently omitted.
  func isUsbDebuggingEnabled() -> Bool {
    return false
  }

  /// The classic Apple-documented `sysctl(KERN_PROC)` technique
  /// (Technical Q&A QA1361): inspect this process's own `kinfo_proc` and
  /// check the `P_TRACED` flag.
  func isDebuggerAttached() -> Bool {
    var info = kinfo_proc()
    var size = MemoryLayout<kinfo_proc>.stride
    var mib: [Int32] = [CTL_KERN, KERN_PROC, KERN_PROC_PID, getpid()]

    let result = sysctl(&mib, UInt32(mib.count), &info, &size, nil, 0)
    guard result == 0 else { return false }

    return (info.kp_proc.p_flag & P_TRACED) != 0
  }

  /// iOS has no direct "mock location provider" concept like Android.
  /// Best-effort: if Core Location has already delivered a location fix
  /// with `sourceInformation.isSimulatedBySoftware` set (iOS 15+), that's a
  /// strong signal; otherwise this conservatively reports `false` rather
  /// than requesting location permission just to check.
  func isMockLocationEnabled() -> Bool {
    #if canImport(CoreLocation) && os(iOS)
    guard #available(iOS 15.0, *) else { return false }
    guard CLLocationManager().authorizationStatus == .authorizedWhenInUse
      || CLLocationManager().authorizationStatus == .authorizedAlways else {
      return false
    }
    return CLLocationManager().location?.sourceInformation?.isSimulatedBySoftware ?? false
    #else
    return false
    #endif
  }
}

```

`ios/Detectors/EmulatorDetector.swift`:

```swift
import Foundation

/// iOS Simulator detection. `targetEnvironment(simulator)` is a compile-time
/// flag resolved per architecture slice, so this is 100% accurate (unlike
/// the Android emulator heuristics, which are runtime signal-based).
final class EmulatorDetector {
  func isDetected() -> Bool {
    #if targetEnvironment(simulator)
    return true
    #else
    return ProcessInfo.processInfo.environment["SIMULATOR_DEVICE_NAME"] != nil
    #endif
  }
}
